Table 1
Representation, Learning Data, and the Social Bias of Machine Learning
1. Q: What does representation mean in machine learning, and why is it the central concern of this chapter?
A: Show answer
A representation is the form in which a system encodes information so that it can process it and produce an output. In a perceptron, an image is represented as pixel values and adjustable weights; in a word-embedding model, a word is represented as a vector of numbers. The chapter also uses representation in a social sense: whose faces, words, experiences, and patterns are present in the data that shape a model. These meanings meet in practice. A technical representation is learned from examples selected from the world, so omissions and distortions in those examples can become systematic limitations or biases in the resulting system.
Study instruction:
- Trace one example from the real-world object or text, through its encoded representation, to the model's prediction. Then identify which people or conditions may be missing from the examples.
Key point:
- A model can only learn from the information made available to it in usable form.
- Technical representations can preserve and reproduce social patterns embedded in their source data.
2. Q: How did Frank Rosenblatt's perceptron classify the cards in the 1958 demonstration?
A: Show answer
The device converted a black-and-white image of a card into 400 binary pixel values from a 20-by-20 image. Each pixel value was multiplied by its own positive or negative weight, and the weighted values were summed. A negative total produced one class, indicating a square on the left; a positive total produced the other, indicating a square on the right. The initial weights were random, so early predictions were unreliable. After repeated corrections, the machine learned to distinguish the two positions, including cards it had not previously seen.
Study instruction:
- Draw the perceptron's input-to-output pipeline: pixels, weights, weighted sum, decision threshold, predicted class. Explain what changes during training.
Key point:
- The perceptron turns an input representation into a decision using adjustable weights.
- Learning occurs when experience changes the parameters that govern future predictions.
3. Q: What are model architecture, parameters, training data, and a training algorithm?
A: Show answer
Architecture describes the model's structure and how information flows through it. Parameters are the adjustable numerical values within that structure; in the perceptron, they are the pixel weights. Training data are the examples used to teach the model, such as labeled cards showing a square on the left or right. The training algorithm is the procedure that adjusts parameters in response to errors. These elements are distinct but interdependent: the architecture limits what the model can express, the data indicate what it should learn, and the algorithm determines how its parameters are updated.
Study instruction:
- For the perceptron and AlexNet, list the architecture, adjustable parameters, training examples, and learning procedure separately.
Key point:
- Architecture defines the model's form; parameters determine its current behavior.
- Data and the optimization procedure jointly shape what the architecture learns.
4. Q: What does it mean for a machine-learning system to learn from experience?
A: Show answer
A system learns from experience when examples cause its internal parameters to change in ways that improve later performance on the task. Rosenblatt described this as a self-induced change in the system's wiring. The important distinction is between a machine that follows only a fully specified set of fixed rules and one whose behavior is adjusted through examples. The system is not necessarily learning concepts in the human sense; it is finding parameter settings that improve a defined objective. Whether the resulting behavior is useful depends on the task, examples, objective, and evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Explain how the perceptron's behavior differs before and after training, without assuming that improvement on its training examples guarantees broad understanding.
Key point:
- Learning is a change in model parameters driven by data and an objective.
- Improved task performance should not automatically be equated with human-like understanding.
5. Q: How does stochastic gradient descent work at a conceptual level?
A: Show answer
Stochastic gradient descent repeatedly selects a training example, compares the model's output with the desired output, and adjusts parameters in a direction expected to reduce the error. 'Stochastic' refers to using a randomly selected example or small sample; 'gradient' refers to the direction of change in the objective; and 'descent' means moving toward a lower error value. The adjustment is usually incremental rather than an attempt to find a perfect parameter setting in one step. The process is repeated across many examples and iterations.
Study instruction:
- Describe one training iteration in order: select an example, predict, calculate error, determine an adjustment direction, update parameters, and repeat.
Key point:
- Training uses repeated small parameter updates to reduce a defined error.
- The objective being optimized determines what counts as an improvement.
6. Q: What is the difference between learning the training examples and generalizing to unseen examples?
A: Show answer
A model performs well on its training examples when it predicts those examples correctly. Generalization means that it also performs reliably on new examples drawn from the conditions in which it will be used. Rosenblatt's demonstration was notable because the perceptron distinguished cards it had not been shown, not merely the cards used during training. A system can memorize peculiarities of its training set and still fail on new cases. Evaluation therefore needs data that were not used to fit the model and that reasonably represent the intended deployment environment.
Study instruction:
- Design a small evaluation plan that keeps some labeled examples separate from training and explains why the held-out examples matter.
Key point:
- Training performance alone is insufficient evidence of reliable generalization.
- Unseen evaluation examples should resemble the real conditions of use.
7. Q: What limitation did Minsky and Papert identify in a single-layer perceptron?
A: Show answer
A single-layer perceptron cannot learn every pattern that may be expressible by a more complex network. The chapter illustrates this with the task of distinguishing cards containing an odd number of squares from cards containing an even number: the model's simple structure cannot represent the required distinction. This was not simply a matter of providing more examples or repeating training for longer. The limitation arose from the architecture itself. Some tasks require intermediate representations and multiple layers of computation.
Study instruction:
- Explain why adding more training examples cannot overcome a task that the model's architecture is incapable of representing.
Key point:
- More data cannot repair every structural limitation in a model.
- Model capacity must match the complexity of the pattern to be learned.
8. Q: Why did multilayer neural networks offer a way beyond the perceptron's limitations?
A: Show answer
A multilayer network can construct intermediate representations of raw inputs and allow later layers to operate on those representations. This makes it possible to express patterns that a single linear decision unit cannot capture. The historical obstacle was not merely inventing additional layers; researchers also needed a practical way to tune the parameters in earlier layers so that the representations they produced would be useful to later layers. The later progress of neural networks depended on methods that made such multilayer training workable.
Study instruction:
- Compare a single-layer classifier with a multilayer network and describe the role of intermediate representations.
Key point:
- Hidden layers allow a model to build progressively more useful representations.
- A deeper architecture is useful only when its parameters can be trained effectively.
9. Q: What does the history of early perceptron research teach about technological optimism?
A: Show answer
The 1958 demonstrations prompted predictions that learning machines might soon walk, talk, see, write, reproduce themselves, or rival human thought. Those expectations went far beyond what the demonstrated classifier could establish. Rosenblatt himself later criticized the exuberance of the press and acknowledged insufficient mathematical rigor in early accounts. The episode shows how a successful prototype can be mistaken for evidence of general capability. Responsible communication should separate what a system has actually demonstrated from speculative future applications.
Study instruction:
- Take one historical claim about the perceptron and classify it as demonstrated capability, plausible research direction, or unsupported prediction.
Key point:
- A narrow success does not establish general intelligence or broad competence.
- Claims about AI should be calibrated to the evidence and scope of testing.
10. Q: Why did neural-network research lose momentum after the 1960s?
A: Show answer
Minsky and Papert's mathematical analysis exposed important limitations of the perceptron, and researchers lacked a reliable method for training the more expressive multilayer networks that might address them. Research enthusiasm, funding, and personnel declined; by the early 1970s, major government support had been withdrawn in the United States and Britain. The episode was shaped by both technical constraints and institutional choices. It should not be simplified into the claim that neural networks had been proved useless: the demonstrated limitations applied to particular model structures and capabilities.
Study instruction:
- Separate the technical reasons for the research slowdown from the funding, institutional, and historical circumstances described in the chapter.
Key point:
- Research setbacks often combine mathematical, computational, and institutional factors.
- A limitation in one model family should not be generalized to every possible architecture.
11. Q: What changed by the 1980s and 1990s that helped neural networks return?
A: Show answer
Researchers developed ways to train networks with multiple layers, making the intermediate representations of deep networks more practically useful. Yann LeCun's work demonstrated that neural networks could identify handwritten numerals, leading to commercial uses such as reading postal codes and processing checks in ATMs. These applications showed that neural networks could deliver value on bounded, structured tasks. Progress was nevertheless constrained by the size of available datasets and the speed of available computers, so the field did not immediately move to large-scale general image recognition.
Study instruction:
- Compare the handwritten-digit applications with the later ImageNet task in terms of task scope, data scale, and computing demands.
Key point:
- Multilayer training enabled useful applications beyond the single-layer perceptron.
- Practical success on narrow tasks does not remove data and computing constraints.
12. Q: Why did neural-network research reach another plateau before the ImageNet breakthrough?
A: Show answer
By the 2000s, researchers understood much of the theoretical promise of large neural networks but lacked enough labeled examples, sufficient computing speed, and the time needed for extensive experiments. Hinton summarized the problem as datasets being thousands of times too small and computers millions of times too slow. A model can be mathematically capable of learning a pattern yet remain impractical to train with the resources available. The later breakthrough required several enabling conditions to improve together rather than a single isolated invention.
Study instruction:
- Create a cause-and-effect diagram linking dataset size, label availability, computing capacity, training time, and model performance.
Key point:
- Theoretical capability is different from practical trainability.
- Large-scale progress depends on data, compute, algorithms, and experimentation working together.
13. Q: How did ImageNet change the environment for computer-vision research?
A: Show answer
ImageNet supplied a large, organized collection of images labeled by human annotators across thousands of categories. The chapter describes a dataset of roughly three million images and more than five thousand categories, built over more than two years and released in 2009. It gave researchers a common resource and a demanding benchmark for comparing image-recognition systems. Its scale made it possible to train and evaluate models on a much broader range of visual examples than earlier datasets had provided.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a large labeled dataset and a shared benchmark can accelerate progress across an entire research community.
Key point:
- Large labeled datasets make broader learning and comparable evaluation possible.
- A benchmark can coordinate research while still reflecting the limitations of its own data.
14. Q: What role did human annotation and services such as Mechanical Turk play in creating large datasets?
A: Show answer
Large image collections often lacked the labels required for supervised training. Crowdsourcing services made it possible to recruit many people to classify images and attach category labels at a scale that would have been difficult for a small research team. This labor converted a large but weakly structured image repository into a usable training resource. The process also creates quality and governance questions: annotators may disagree, instructions may be ambiguous, and low-paid repetitive work can be difficult to supervise. The quality of the resulting dataset depends on how the labeling work is designed and checked.
Study instruction:
- Design an annotation protocol for an image dataset, including label definitions, disagreement handling, quality checks, and worker guidance.
Key point:
- Human labeling is part of the technical infrastructure of supervised learning.
- Annotation scale must be matched by quality assurance and responsible labor practices.
15. Q: Why were GPUs especially useful for training neural networks?
A: Show answer
Graphics processing units were designed to perform many relatively simple calculations in parallel to render visual scenes. Neural-network training also involves large numbers of repeated numerical operations, so this hardware was well suited to the workload. GPUs allowed researchers to complete calculations much faster than relying only on conventional processors, reducing the time needed to test and train large models. The AlexNet story shows how hardware developed for one industry can become a crucial enabling technology in another.
Study instruction:
- Compare the type of work GPUs were designed for with the repeated numerical operations involved in neural-network training.
Key point:
- Parallel hardware can make computationally intensive learning practical.
- A technology developed for one purpose may enable major advances in another field.
16. Q: Why was the 2012 AlexNet result a turning point in computer vision?
A: Show answer
AlexNet substantially outperformed the competing systems in the ImageNet challenge, making roughly half as many errors as the second-place model, as described in the chapter. The result demonstrated that a large multilayer neural network trained with abundant labeled images and GPU computation could produce a major improvement in visual recognition. Its significance was not just that one model won a competition. It changed expectations about the practical effectiveness of deep learning and encouraged a broader shift in computer-vision research. The network had roughly 650,000 neurons, eight layers, and 60 million adjustable weights, illustrating the scale of the advance.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the elements that made AlexNet important and distinguish the benchmark result from claims about universal visual understanding.
Key point:
- AlexNet demonstrated the practical power of deep learning at scale.
- A benchmark breakthrough is evidence about a defined task, not every visual task.
17. Q: What is data augmentation, and why did it help AlexNet?
A: Show answer
Data augmentation creates altered versions of existing training examples so that the model encounters a wider range of plausible appearances. The chapter describes mirror images, slight crops, and color changes. These transformations were useful because an object should often remain the same category when viewed from a slightly different position or under different lighting. Augmentation can therefore encourage a model to learn features that are stable across such variations instead of depending too heavily on one exact image. It does not create wholly new real-world evidence, however, and transformations must preserve the correct label.
Study instruction:
- For an image-classification task, list transformations that should preserve the label and transformations that could change the object's identity or meaning.
Key point:
- Augmentation teaches robustness to selected variations in the input.
- A transformation is useful only when its label-preserving assumption is valid.
18. Q: What is dropout, and what problem was it intended to address?
A: Show answer
Dropout randomly turns off selected portions of a neural network during training. This prevents the model from relying too heavily on a fixed combination of units and can encourage more robust internal representations. In the AlexNet work described in the chapter, Krizhevsky tested Hinton's dropout idea and found that it helped. Dropout is a training technique, not a guarantee against every form of overfitting or bias. Its effect must be evaluated for the specific model and task.
Study instruction:
- Explain how randomly disabling units during training could discourage brittle dependence on particular internal pathways.
Key point:
- Dropout is a regularization technique used to improve robustness during training.
- Its benefit should be confirmed through appropriate validation rather than assumed.
19. Q: What contribution did rectified linear units make in the AlexNet experiments?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that Krizhevsky experimented with neurons using a rectified linear output function and found that this choice helped. A rectified linear unit, commonly called ReLU, outputs zero for negative input and passes positive input through. This simple nonlinear behavior helps a multilayer network model more complex relationships than a purely linear system could. The broader lesson is that architecture and activation-function choices can materially affect how effectively a network trains.
Study instruction:
- Describe the basic behavior of ReLU and explain why nonlinear activation is important in a multilayer model.
Key point:
- Nonlinear activation functions let stacked layers express richer patterns.
- A seemingly small design choice can affect training speed and model performance.
20. Q: Why is the Google Photos 'gorillas' incident primarily a training-data warning in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The image classifier repeatedly assigned a profoundly inappropriate label to photographs of two Black men. The chapter emphasizes that the underlying learning procedure was a generic optimization method performing the task it had been given; the deeper failure lay in the examples from which it had learned. If people with darker skin are poorly represented in training images, the system may perform badly on them or map unfamiliar appearances to unsuitable categories. The incident shows why a model's errors must be investigated through its data and design context, not blamed reflexively on an abstract algorithm.
Study instruction:
- For a classification failure, investigate the training examples, label quality, subgroup coverage, task definition, and deployment context before assigning a cause.
Key point:
- A standard learning algorithm can still produce harmful outcomes from unrepresentative data.
- Error analysis must examine the data and objective as well as the algorithm.
21. Q: How should algorithmic bias be distinguished from bias in training data?
A: Show answer
Algorithmic bias refers to systematic effects arising from the model's procedure, objective, or design choices. Data bias arises when the examples or labels used to train or evaluate the model systematically omit, distort, or overrepresent parts of the world. The two can interact, and a real failure may involve both. In the Google Photos example, the chapter's central diagnosis is that the system learned from an imbalanced collection of examples. The distinction is useful for investigation, but it should not become an excuse to ignore model design or organizational responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Create a diagnostic checklist that tests data, labels, architecture, objective, thresholds, and deployment decisions as separate possible contributors to a disparity.
Key point:
- Bias can enter through data, objectives, architecture, and deployment choices.
- A sound diagnosis identifies the contributing mechanisms rather than using one broad label.
22. Q: What is the 'Shirley card' analogy, and what does it reveal about calibration?
A: Show answer
For decades, photographic film was calibrated using reference images dominated by White models. Exposure and color-processing choices were consequently optimized around a narrow range of skin tones, producing poor results for many Black subjects. The chapter uses this history as an analogy for machine-learning training sets: a reference collection silently establishes what the system treats as normal. Calibration is therefore not purely technical. The selection of reference examples determines whose appearance is handled well and whose is treated as an edge case.
Study instruction:
- Identify a modern technology that relies on a reference sample or benchmark, and ask whose characteristics that reference treats as typical.
Key point:
- Calibration data embed assumptions about what a normal input looks like.
- A narrow reference population can produce systematic disadvantages for others.
23. Q: How did photographic design choices show that bias can exist inside a technical apparatus?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that camera and film systems were not neutral windows onto the world. Their chemistry, exposure practices, and calibration procedures were tuned using particular reference subjects, so some skin tones were reproduced less accurately. This illustrates a broader point: technical systems embody design choices, defaults, and assumptions that may be invisible to users. A device can appear objective while performing differently across groups because its underlying design was optimized around a limited population.
Study instruction:
- Map the hidden design assumptions in a familiar technical product, including its calibration references, defaults, and populations used in testing.
Key point:
- Technology reflects the choices and reference conditions used to design it.
- Apparently neutral devices can distribute performance unevenly.
24. Q: Why does representation in a training dataset matter even when the dataset is very large?
A: Show answer
A large dataset can still be unrepresentative. If one population contributes many examples and another contributes very few, the model receives unequal evidence about the people and conditions it will encounter. More data can improve average performance while leaving gaps for underrepresented groups. The relevant questions are not only how many records exist, but who and what they depict, how they were collected, which labels were applied, and whether the distribution resembles the intended use environment.
Study instruction:
- Audit a hypothetical dataset by calculating subgroup counts and comparing them with the population and operating conditions the model is intended to serve.
Key point:
- Dataset size does not guarantee dataset coverage or fairness.
- Representation must be assessed in relation to the intended population and task.
25. Q: What did Joy Buolamwini's experiences with facial-recognition projects demonstrate?
A: Show answer
Buolamwini encountered systems that did not reliably recognize her face, including a robot project and an augmented-reality mirror that worked when she wore a white mask. These experiences revealed that apparently general-purpose face software could fail for a user whose skin tone was poorly represented in its development data. Her subsequent research turned an individual failure into a systematic investigation of commercial systems. The episode illustrates why unusual user experiences can be important evidence of a broader design or data problem.
Study instruction:
- Turn a single reported failure into a testable research question: define the affected group, system behavior, comparison groups, and evidence needed.
Key point:
- A user's failure experience can reveal a systemic performance gap.
- Testing must include the people who will actually use or be affected by the system.
26. Q: What did the Labeled Faces in the Wild dataset audit reveal?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that a later analysis found the Labeled Faces in the Wild dataset was more than 77 percent male and more than 83 percent White. It also notes that George W. Bush appeared in 530 unique images, more than twice the number of images of all Black women combined. The original dataset's creators had acknowledged technical limitations such as lighting, but its demographic composition received much less attention until later. The case shows that a dataset can become influential before its social coverage is adequately examined.
Study instruction:
- Review a dataset card or inventory and record demographic composition, collection source, duplicate concentration, known exclusions, and stated limitations.
Key point:
- A widely used benchmark can carry serious representation gaps.
- Dataset audits should examine social composition as well as technical image quality.
27. Q: What did the IJB-A dataset analysis add to the discussion of representation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a face-image dataset released with a claim of wider geographic variation. Buolamwini and Gebru's analysis nevertheless found that it was more than 75 percent male and almost 80 percent light-skinned, with dark-skinned females making up only 4.4 percent. The example demonstrates that a dataset's stated goal or label does not establish that it has achieved balanced representation. Independent analysis is needed to test claims about diversity and to identify intersections that may remain poorly covered.
Study instruction:
- Compare a dataset's advertised purpose with measured subgroup representation. Note any groups that remain scarce despite the dataset's stated diversity goals.
Key point:
- Dataset diversity claims require empirical verification.
- Intersectional groups may remain underrepresented even when broad diversity is claimed.
28. Q: How did Buolamwini and Gebru design a more balanced evaluation dataset?
A: Show answer
They assembled images of representatives from the parliaments of Rwanda, Senegal, South Africa, Iceland, Finland, and Sweden. This approach produced a dataset that was notably more balanced by skin tone and gender than earlier face datasets. It was not balanced on every dimension: subjects were mostly middle-aged or older, centered in the frame, and looking toward the camera under relatively limited variation in pose and lighting. The design illustrates a practical trade-off: improving coverage on selected dimensions does not automatically make a dataset representative of every real-world condition.
Study instruction:
- Design a sampling plan that states which dimensions are balanced, which remain limited, and how those limits affect the conclusions that can be drawn.
Key point:
- Dataset design should make its coverage goals and exclusions explicit.
- Improvement on one dimension does not establish universal representativeness.
29. Q: What did the commercial face-classification comparison reveal about intersectional error?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that three commercial systems achieved roughly 90 percent overall gender-classification accuracy on the evaluation dataset, yet each was about 10 to 20 percentage points more accurate on male faces than female faces and on lighter faces than darker faces. The largest gap appeared for dark-skinned women. In the reported IBM results, the error rate was 0.3 percent for light-skinned men and 34.7 percent for dark-skinned women. These are findings for the systems and test set studied at that time, not timeless estimates of all current products.
Study instruction:
- Recreate a subgroup error table for a hypothetical classifier and compare aggregate accuracy with the worst-performing intersectional group.
Key point:
- Aggregate scores can hide severe subgroup disparities.
- Intersectional evaluation can reveal failures missed by one-dimensional comparisons.
30. Q: What is intersectional analysis in machine-learning evaluation?
A: Show answer
Intersectional analysis examines combinations of characteristics rather than treating each characteristic in isolation. A system may perform differently by gender and by skin tone, but the combination of dark skin and female presentation can expose a much larger error rate than either broad category suggests. This matters because people experience multiple characteristics simultaneously, and a model's errors may concentrate at their intersections. Evaluation should therefore consider the groups relevant to the use case, while avoiding unsupported assumptions about how people identify or how categories should be defined.
Study instruction:
- Choose two or more relevant dimensions for a hypothetical system and specify why their intersections should be tested, not just their separate averages.
Key point:
- Performance disparities can compound or become visible at group intersections.
- Evaluation categories should be justified by the task and the affected population.
31. Q: Why is a claim such as '99 percent accurate' incomplete without further information?
A: Show answer
An accuracy figure is meaningful only when the task, dataset, population, error definition, and evaluation method are known. A high aggregate score can coexist with serious failures for a small subgroup, especially when that subgroup is rare in the test set. Decision-makers should ask which examples were tested, who was represented, what types of mistakes were counted, and whether the operating environment matches the benchmark. A single headline number cannot establish suitability for a high-impact decision.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a generic accuracy claim as a complete evaluation statement that identifies the task, sample, metric, subgroup results, and limitations.
Key point:
- Accuracy is conditional on the data and metric used to measure it.
- Subgroup results and error types are essential for judging real-world suitability.
32. Q: What should a responsible audit of a machine-learning training dataset examine?
A: Show answer
A useful audit should examine the collection method, source population, subgroup representation, label definitions, annotation process, missing data, duplicates, and conditions under which examples were captured. It should also record what the dataset excludes and whether those exclusions matter for the intended application. For a face dataset, relevant dimensions may include skin tone, gender presentation, age, lighting, pose, and image quality. The audit should be repeated when the data are refreshed or the system is moved to a new population or environment.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a dataset-audit template with fields for provenance, population coverage, labels, quality checks, known gaps, intended use, and review date.
Key point:
- Dataset documentation should describe both what is present and what is absent.
- Coverage must be reassessed when data or deployment conditions change.
33. Q: What is the distributional hypothesis in computational linguistics?
A: Show answer
The distributional hypothesis holds that words appearing in similar contexts tend to have related meanings or uses. Instead of requiring a machine to understand a word through direct experience of the world, the model can infer patterns from the words that surround it in large collections of text. The chapter uses the idea to explain why statistical language models can learn useful relationships from context alone. It is a powerful approximation, but contextual similarity is not identical to full human understanding, grounded experience, or correct interpretation in every situation.
Study instruction:
- Choose several words and compare the contexts in which they commonly appear. Identify which similarities are plausible and which might be misleading.
Key point:
- Contextual patterns provide a statistical route to representing word relationships.
- Distributional similarity is useful but does not equal complete semantic understanding.
34. Q: Why did early n-gram language models struggle as more context was included?
A: Show answer
N-gram methods count sequences of adjacent words, such as pairs or triples, and use their observed frequencies to predict a missing word. Adding more context can improve a prediction when the exact sequence has been seen, but the number of possible sequences grows rapidly. Many combinations never appear in the corpus, while others occur only once or twice. The resulting data become sparse, making reliable estimates difficult. A model may fail on a plausible phrase simply because that exact sequence was absent from its stored counts.
Study instruction:
- Construct a small bigram and trigram table from a short text and observe how longer sequences become less frequent and more sparse.
Key point:
- Longer n-grams provide more context but require many more observations.
- A phrase not seen before may still be linguistically plausible.
35. Q: What is the curse of dimensionality in the chapter's discussion of language models?
A: Show answer
The curse of dimensionality describes the difficulty of working in a space whose number of possible combinations grows rapidly as more dimensions or contextual features are added. In n-gram language modeling, longer word sequences create an enormous set of possible phrases, most of which are not observed in a finite corpus. This makes simple frequency counts unreliable for many combinations. The chapter presents distributed representations as a way to generalize across related words and contexts rather than storing every possible phrase independently.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a finite corpus cannot contain every possible phrase and how a representation that shares statistical structure can help generalize.
Key point:
- Sparse observations become more severe as the space of possible combinations expands.
- Generalization requires learning shared structure rather than relying only on exact phrase counts.
36. Q: How do distributed representations and word embeddings work?
A: Show answer
A distributed representation maps a word to a vector of numerical coordinates in an abstract space. Words used in similar contexts tend to receive vectors that are near one another, allowing the model to express degrees of similarity rather than treating every word as an unrelated symbol. The chapter describes word2vec representations with hundreds of numerical dimensions. The vectors are learned through a prediction objective: the model adjusts them so that words become useful for predicting the contexts in which they occur. The resulting geometry can support many language-processing tasks.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simplified two-dimensional map of related words and explain what proximity means and what it does not prove.
Key point:
- Word embeddings encode contextual relationships as numerical vectors.
- Vector geometry provides a reusable representation for downstream language tasks.
37. Q: How does stochastic gradient descent help create word embeddings?
A: Show answer
A model begins with word vectors that do not yet encode useful relationships. It samples a phrase, hides or predicts a word from its context, and compares its prediction with the observed text. When the prediction is wrong, the vectors are adjusted so that the correct word becomes more compatible with the surrounding context and incorrect alternatives become less compatible. Repeating this process across a large corpus gradually organizes the vectors into a space that captures statistical regularities of language.
Study instruction:
- Describe one word-prediction update and identify which vectors are moved closer or farther apart and why.
Key point:
- Embeddings emerge through repeated optimization of a context-prediction task.
- The model's learned geometry reflects the patterns and limitations of its corpus.
38. Q: Why were word embeddings useful beyond predicting missing words?
A: Show answer
Once words are represented as vectors, a system can compare their distances and combine vector relationships to support other tasks. The chapter describes applications such as ranking search results, translating text, and analyzing sentiment in reviews. Vector arithmetic can also produce analogies that appear to capture geography, grammar, professions, or other relationships. These capabilities made embeddings broadly useful, but their flexibility also made them easy to reuse in settings for which their assumptions and biases had not been carefully evaluated.
Study instruction:
- List three downstream uses of word embeddings and identify what validation each use would require before deployment.
Key point:
- A representation learned for one objective can influence many downstream applications.
- Transfer to a new task requires separate evaluation of risks and suitability.
39. Q: What do word-vector analogies reveal about the structure learned by embeddings?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives examples in which vector differences appear to encode relationships, such as a country and its capital or a grammatical change from one form to another. This suggests that the model has organized some recurring relationships into directions within its numerical space. The analogies are striking because the model was trained to predict contextual words, not explicitly instructed to learn geography or grammar. However, an analogy is evidence of a statistical regularity in the corpus, not proof that the model has acquired a complete or reliable concept of the relationship.
Study instruction:
- Test a few analogy patterns on a small conceptual example and distinguish a useful regularity from an unsupported claim of understanding.
Key point:
- Vector arithmetic can expose relationships captured in language statistics.
- An analogy's plausibility does not establish the model's broader understanding.
40. Q: How can word embeddings encode gender stereotypes?
A: Show answer
If a corpus repeatedly associates certain professions, activities, names, or writing styles with one gender, an embedding model can encode those associations in its vector space. The chapter describes examples in which professions are linked to stereotyped activities or roles, such as associating carpentry with men and sewing with women. The model is not required to endorse those associations; it reproduces patterns that help it predict words in the training text. When such vectors are used for ranking or classification, descriptive correlations can become consequential decisions.
Study instruction:
- Identify a stereotype that might be learned from text and trace how it could affect a downstream ranking or recommendation system.
Key point:
- Embeddings can preserve stereotypes even when the training objective contains no explicit social rule.
- A predictive correlation can become harmful when reused for consequential decisions.
41. Q: How could a résumé-ranking system reproduce gender bias without using an explicit gender field?
A: Show answer
A system can learn correlations between job titles and names, hobbies, schools, locations, vocabulary, or writing patterns that are associated with gender in historical data. Even if the explicit gender field is removed, these proxy features may still carry related information. The chapter illustrates this with the possibility that an otherwise identical résumé bearing a male-associated name could rank higher because the embedding treats that name as more typical of engineering résumés. Removing one visible variable therefore does not guarantee that the model has become blind to the underlying characteristic.
Study instruction:
- List at least five possible proxy variables for a protected or sensitive characteristic in a hypothetical hiring dataset and explain how each could influence ranking.
Key point:
- Removing a direct attribute does not remove information correlated with it.
- Fairness testing must examine indirect pathways and proxy features.
42. Q: What does the orchestral blind-audition example teach about proxy variables?
A: Show answer
Orchestras introduced screens so judges could hear performers without seeing them, reducing the influence of visible identity cues. The chapter notes that some orchestras also asked performers to remove their shoes because footsteps could reveal information about them. The example shows that concealing one signal may leave other correlated signals available. Machine-learning systems can exploit far more subtle proxies than a visible name or face, including word choice, educational history, or patterns in a document. Fairness cannot be established simply by deleting one column.
Study instruction:
- For a blind-screening process, identify what the screen hides, what information may still leak, and what additional controls would be needed.
Key point:
- A single blindfold can leave indirect identifying information intact.
- Proxy analysis should consider the full feature set and data-generation process.
43. Q: What happened with Amazon's experimental résumé-screening tool, and why is it significant?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experimental tool trained to rank applicants by similarity to résumés from people Amazon had hired in the previous decade. Because those historical hires were predominantly men, the model learned patterns that disadvantaged some women-associated terms, including references to women's activities and colleges. Attempts to remove visible terms did not eliminate subtler correlations in vocabulary. The project was ultimately abandoned. The case demonstrates how historical hiring decisions can become a model's definition of merit when similarity to past hires is used as the target.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a hypothetical hiring model trained on historical employee profiles. Identify how past selection patterns could become embedded as a future ranking rule.
Key point:
- Historical outcomes are not automatically a fair or valid target for future decisions.
- A model can scale past discrimination even when protected attributes are not explicit inputs.
44. Q: Why is 'similarity to successful past employees' a risky objective for recruitment?
A: Show answer
The objective assumes that the people previously hired provide an appropriate template for future talent. If historical hiring was shaped by unequal opportunity, narrow networks, or biased evaluation, similarity scoring can reproduce those patterns. It may also confuse traits associated with past selection with the capabilities actually required for the job. A better recruitment design begins with job-relevant criteria, validates them against performance and fairness evidence, and subjects automated rankings to scrutiny. Historical data can inform analysis, but should not be treated as a neutral definition of merit.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a similarity-based hiring objective as a set of job-relevant criteria and specify how each criterion would be validated.
Key point:
- Past selection patterns may encode historical bias rather than job necessity.
- Recruitment objectives should be tied to defensible job requirements and tested outcomes.
45. Q: What was the goal of the word-embedding debiasing approach discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The researchers sought to reduce inappropriate gender associations while preserving useful distinctions, such as relationships between words that are genuinely gender-specific. Simply deleting every trace of gender could damage meaningful language relationships. The task was therefore not to erase all gender information indiscriminately, but to identify which associations were appropriate for the intended use and which reflected stereotypes that should not influence downstream decisions. This framing makes debiasing a problem of judgment and trade-offs, not merely a mechanical deletion of one variable.
Study instruction:
- Explain why removing every gender-related distinction could damage legitimate meanings while leaving stereotypes untreated could perpetuate harm.
Key point:
- Debiasing aims to reduce harmful associations without destroying useful information.
- The meaning of an association depends on context and application.
46. Q: How did principal component analysis help identify a gender direction in word embeddings?
A: Show answer
The researchers considered several pairs of words that differed by gender, such as woman and man or she and he. Rather than relying on a single pair, they used principal component analysis to identify a direction that captured the greatest shared variation across the selected differences. That direction served as an approximate gender axis in the vector space. It was a practical statistical construction, not proof that gender has one universal numerical dimension. The result depended on the word pairs selected and the language model being examined.
Study instruction:
- Explain why using several word pairs may provide a more stable direction than defining gender from one pair alone, and identify the assumptions involved.
Key point:
- A statistical axis summarizes patterns in selected examples; it is not a complete theory of gender.
- The result depends on the model, vocabulary, and choices used to define the axis.
47. Q: Why was it difficult to decide which words should retain gender associations?
A: Show answer
Some words have clear gender-related meanings, such as pronouns or certain anatomical terms, while many others depend on social and institutional context. The chapter discusses words such as nurse, whose occupational meaning is not inherently gendered, and rabbi, whose gender implications may depend on the religious denomination. The researchers consulted social scientists because a purely technical team could not determine a universally correct list of acceptable associations. The classification required social judgment and could vary by application and culture.
Study instruction:
- Choose five words with potentially gendered associations and explain what contextual evidence would be needed before deciding whether those associations are appropriate.
Key point:
- The boundary between relevant and stereotypical gender information is context-dependent.
- Fairness definitions require social and domain expertise, not only mathematical methods.
48. Q: How did the researchers evaluate whether their debiased embeddings reduced stereotypes?
A: Show answer
They asked human annotators recruited through Mechanical Turk to judge whether selected analogies reflected stereotypes. The chapter reports that 19 percent of analogies from the original model were judged stereotypical, compared with 6 percent from the debiased model. This provided evidence of improvement on the evaluated examples, but it did not establish that all harmful associations had been removed. The wording of the questions and the judgments of the annotators mattered, so evaluation design itself required care and consultation with social scientists.
Study instruction:
- Design a human-evaluation protocol for stereotype reduction, including clear instructions, diverse annotators, disagreement analysis, and limits on generalization.
Key point:
- Human evaluation can test social meaning that a purely technical metric may miss.
- A favorable test result applies to the examples and criteria actually evaluated.
49. Q: What trade-off can arise when gender associations are removed from word embeddings?
A: Show answer
Reducing gender associations may also remove distinctions that are useful or semantically appropriate. The chapter gives the example that a debiased model might treat 'grandmothered in' as equally likely as 'grandfathered in,' illustrating a change in language regularity. A designer must therefore consider how much predictive performance or linguistic nuance is acceptable to trade for reduced stereotyping. That trade-off cannot be resolved by accuracy alone; the consequences depend on the application and the harms being addressed.
Study instruction:
- Create a trade-off table for one proposed debiasing intervention, recording the stereotype reduction sought, useful information at risk, and application-specific consequences.
Key point:
- Debiasing can change useful as well as harmful associations.
- Trade-offs must be evaluated in the context of the decision the model will support.
50. Q: Why did later research question whether word-embedding debiasing had fully solved the problem?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes later work arguing that removing an explicit gender direction may leave many implicit associations intact. A model might stop connecting a profession directly to words such as woman or she while retaining relationships among occupations that were stereotypically associated with women. This can make a model appear fairer on the most visible test while leaving deeper structure largely unchanged. Debiasing should therefore be treated as an intervention with measurable limits, not as a certification that a representation is free of bias.
Study instruction:
- Design a follow-up audit that tests both direct gender associations and indirect relationships among occupations, skills, names, and other terms.
Key point:
- Removing an obvious bias signal may leave correlated patterns untouched.
- Debiasing claims require broad and repeated evaluation.
51. Q: What does the implicit association test contribute to understanding machine-learning bias?
A: Show answer
The implicit association test uses response times to examine how readily people connect concepts, such as names associated with different racial groups and positive or negative words. The chapter describes research finding that distances between word embeddings corresponded with patterns in human reaction-time data. This suggests that embeddings can capture associations present in human language and social cognition. It does not mean that a vector model has human beliefs or that a reaction-time test diagnoses an individual person's character. The comparison is evidence about patterns, not a complete explanation of their causes.
Study instruction:
- Explain what a reaction-time association test measures and distinguish a group-level statistical pattern from a claim about an individual's beliefs.
Key point:
- Machine representations can reflect associations also observed in human social cognition.
- Similarity between measures does not establish identical mechanisms or causal explanations.
52. Q: What is the difference between stereotypical bias and a statistical reflection of existing social patterns?
A: Show answer
Some associations in text may reflect stereotypes or unjust assumptions; others may correlate with measurable patterns in the world, such as the current gender distribution of an occupation. The chapter discusses research comparing word embeddings with census and labor statistics, finding that occupational gender associations in the vectors correlated with workforce composition. This does not make the associations normatively appropriate. A model may accurately describe an unequal status quo while still being unsuitable as a rule for deciding who should receive an opportunity.
Study instruction:
- For one observed correlation, state separately whether it is descriptively accurate, normatively acceptable, and appropriate to use in a decision.
Key point:
- Descriptive accuracy does not establish fairness or justify a decision rule.
- A model of the status quo should not automatically become a prescription for the future.
53. Q: How can word embeddings be used as a tool for studying social change over time?
A: Show answer
Researchers can train or analyze representations using text from different periods and compare how words relate to one another across time. The chapter describes work using historical text to study changes in gender and racial associations, including shifts around major social and political events. These patterns can provide a quantitative view of changes in public language and cultural attitudes. However, the results depend on which texts survive, which communities publish, and how word meanings change, so embeddings are a lens on recorded discourse rather than a complete census of society.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a longitudinal study using text from several decades. Identify how corpus composition, changing meanings, and historical context could affect interpretation.
Key point:
- Temporal embeddings can help measure changing associations in recorded language.
- The corpus is a partial record of society, not a neutral or complete mirror.
54. Q: What are the limits of using embeddings as a real-time dashboard of social attitudes?
A: Show answer
A changing vector association may signal a shift in public discourse, but it can also reflect changes in the volume, source, or composition of available text. Online publishing is not evenly distributed across communities, and a sudden event can temporarily dominate language. A dashboard would therefore need stable sampling methods, transparent time windows, uncertainty measures, and contextual interpretation. It should be used as a descriptive indicator that prompts investigation, not as a direct measure of what every person believes or how institutions should act.
Study instruction:
- Specify controls for a proposed language-bias dashboard, including source balance, sampling consistency, event effects, uncertainty, and human interpretation.
Key point:
- A language dashboard measures patterns in sampled text, not society in its entirety.
- Descriptive indicators need context before they support policy or operational decisions.
55. Q: How can machine-learning models create feedback loops that intensify the bias they learned?
A: Show answer
A deployed model can influence the decisions and records that later become its training data. For example, if a résumé-ranking system repeatedly promotes male applicants because of historical patterns, more men may be hired and therefore appear in the next dataset of successful employees. The model may then learn an even stronger association between men and the target role. The prediction changes the world being measured, so future data are no longer independent observations of the original environment. Without intervention, a modest initial skew can become increasingly difficult to reverse.
Study instruction:
- Draw a feedback-loop diagram for an automated selection system, showing how predictions affect outcomes, records, and the next training cycle.
Key point:
- Deployment can change the data distribution on which future models learn.
- Monitoring must test whether model decisions are amplifying the patterns they detect.
56. Q: Why should models used for consequential decisions be deployed cautiously and reviewed over time?
A: Show answer
A model can remain in production long after its training data, social context, or assumptions have changed. The chapter warns that deployed systems may persist for decades, and that feedback loops can make early errors harder to correct. A responsible approach includes pre-deployment subgroup testing, clear limits on use, monitoring of outcomes, review when populations or policies change, and a process for correcting harmful effects. The level of scrutiny should reflect the consequences of error and the difficulty of reversing a decision.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a lifecycle review plan covering pre-deployment testing, approval, ongoing monitoring, incident response, retraining, and retirement criteria.
Key point:
- Model risk continues after launch; deployment is not the end of evaluation.
- Early intervention is often easier than correcting entrenched feedback effects.
57. Q: How can the same biased model be used descriptively rather than prescriptively?
A: Show answer
A model that captures social associations may be useful as a diagnostic instrument for identifying patterns in historical or current language. It becomes dangerous when those same associations are treated as a legitimate basis for deciding who should be hired, promoted, served, or believed. Descriptive use asks what patterns are present and how they change; prescriptive use turns those patterns into rules for action. The chapter argues that models can help make diffuse biases visible, provided users do not mistake a statistical portrait of the world for a standard of how the world ought to be.
Study instruction:
- Take one model output and write two interpretations: one that describes a measured pattern and another that would improperly turn the pattern into a decision rule.
Key point:
- A statistical mirror can support diagnosis without being a legitimate decision policy.
- Descriptive evidence should not be converted into normative rules without independent justification.
58. Q: Why does responsible machine-learning work require collaboration between technical and social disciplines?
A: Show answer
Technical researchers can measure model behavior and design optimization methods, but questions about discrimination, acceptable trade-offs, and social meaning often require expertise beyond computer science. The chapter describes researchers consulting sociologists and linguists when deciding how to define gender associations and interpret language patterns. Social scientists, in turn, can use machine-learning representations as tools for studying cultural change. Collaboration helps expose assumptions that a single discipline may overlook and connects model design to the people and institutions affected by it.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed high-impact model, identify the technical, legal, social-science, operational, and affected-community expertise needed before deployment.
Key point:
- Fairness and meaning cannot be settled by engineering metrics alone.
- Interdisciplinary review connects technical performance with social consequences.
59. Q: What practical safeguards follow from the chapter's analysis of representation and bias?
A: Show answer
A practical program should document dataset provenance and composition, test performance across relevant subgroups and intersections, inspect labels and proxies, and evaluate the system in conditions resembling actual use. It should also define the decision the model is allowed to support, retain human review for consequential cases, monitor outcomes after deployment, and provide a way to investigate and correct errors. These are operational recommendations synthesized from the chapter's cases; they are not a substitute for applicable law, professional standards, or organization-specific governance requirements.
Study instruction:
- Turn the safeguards into an implementation checklist with an accountable owner, evidence required, review frequency, and escalation route for each control.
Key point:
- Responsible representation requires controls across data, evaluation, deployment, and oversight.
- Governance should be specific enough to assign responsibility and verify performance.
60. Q: What is the chapter's central lesson about the relationship between representation and society?
A: Show answer
Machine-learning systems learn patterns from representations of the world, but those representations are created through human choices about what to collect, label, measure, and optimize. The resulting models can reproduce social inequalities, reveal them, or amplify them through automated decisions and feedback. Their technical power does not remove their dependence on human values and institutions. The chapter's central challenge is therefore not only to build models that predict accurately, but to understand whose experiences shape the model and what happens when its predictions influence real lives.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter as a chain from social world to dataset, representation, prediction, decision, and feedback. Mark where human choices and controls enter.
Key point:
- Models inherit patterns from the data and design choices that shape them.
- The consequences of representation depend on how predictions are used in society.
Rows: 60
Table 2
Fairness in Predictive Systems and Algorithmic Decision-Making
1. Q: Why did early researchers propose statistical prediction to improve parole decisions?
A: Show answer
The proposal arose from concern that parole decisions were inconsistent, subjective, and vulnerable to political influence. In the 1920s, Illinois parole chairman Hinton Clabaugh commissioned a broad study to examine whether evidence about prisoners and their circumstances could help the board make more systematic decisions. Ernest Burgess analyzed thousands of parole cases and identified factors associated with success or failure after release. The argument was not that a formula could determine an individual's future with certainty. It was that carefully studied patterns across many cases could inform decisions more consistently than unstructured impressions alone. The chapter presents this as an early example of using quantitative methods to improve a consequential public decision.
Study instruction:
- Compare a discretionary parole decision with a decision informed by documented evidence. Identify what each approach can reveal and what each can miss.
Key point:
- Statistical tools were proposed partly to reduce inconsistent human judgment.
- Prediction was intended as decision support, not certainty.
2. Q: What did Ernest Burgess's Illinois parole study attempt to establish?
A: Show answer
Burgess examined roughly three thousand Illinois parolees and investigated whether recorded characteristics were associated with later parole success or violation. His analysis considered criminal history, employment, residence, offense type, sentence length, time served, psychiatric diagnoses, and other information. He also explored whether political influence affected parole decisions. The study challenged the assumption that human behavior was wholly unpredictable and argued that meaningful group-level patterns could be identified. Its categories and social classifications reflected the period in which it was conducted and should not be treated as modern scientific or ethical standards. Its enduring significance in the chapter is the attempt to make parole decisions more evidence-informed and reviewable.
Study instruction:
- List the kinds of variables Burgess considered and distinguish potentially relevant evidence from dated or problematic classifications.
Key point:
- Burgess looked for patterns across cases rather than relying only on impressions.
- Historical classifications must be assessed critically in their social and scientific context.
3. Q: Why did Burgess emphasize group-level prediction rather than certainty about an individual?
A: Show answer
Burgess recognized that a statistical pattern cannot determine exactly what one person will do. A factor associated with a higher or lower violation rate across a substantial group does not prove that a particular individual will behave in the predicted way. His proposal was to summarize the observed rates for relevant factors so parole board members could consider them alongside the individual case. This distinction matters because probability describes uncertainty across cases; it does not establish destiny for a person. Treating a group-level estimate as a personal certainty can turn a decision aid into an unjustified label and obscure the individual's circumstances, capacity for change, and rights.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a group-level risk statement so that it communicates probability and uncertainty without describing an individual as destined to offend.
Key point:
- A statistical association is not an individual guarantee.
- Risk estimates should inform judgment without replacing individualized consideration.
4. Q: What weakness in discretionary human parole decisions did statistical tools seek to address?
A: Show answer
Unstructured discretion can produce inconsistent outcomes because decision-makers may weigh similar facts differently, rely on memorable anecdotes, respond to political pressure, or be influenced by impressions formed during a hearing. Two people with comparable circumstances may therefore receive different decisions. A structured instrument can make the factors being considered more explicit and allow patterns in decisions to be reviewed. However, consistency alone does not guarantee justice. A model can formalize biased historical practices, omit important context, or create false confidence. The relevant comparison is not between a flawed algorithm and an ideal human decision-maker, but between specific decision processes, their evidence, their error patterns, and their accountability.
Study instruction:
- Identify two possible sources of inconsistency in human judgment and two possible sources of error in a statistical model.
Key point:
- Structure can make decisions more consistent and reviewable.
- Consistency is not the same as fairness or correctness.
5. Q: How did the early Illinois parole experience influence later risk-assessment systems?
A: Show answer
The Illinois work helped establish the idea that information about criminal history and personal circumstances could be organized into a repeatable assessment. The chapter describes the later publication of a parole prediction manual and even an early discussion of machine methods, including punch-card equipment, to process records and produce individual summaries. Adoption was initially slow, but the broader spread of computers and statistical techniques eventually made risk instruments more common in criminal justice. This history shows that algorithmic decision support did not suddenly appear with modern artificial intelligence. Its roots lie in earlier efforts to quantify uncertainty and standardize administrative judgments.
Study instruction:
- Create a short timeline from Burgess's study through the later expansion of computerized risk assessment.
Key point:
- Quantified parole assessment predates modern machine learning.
- Automation changed the scale and speed of an older statistical practice.
6. Q: What is COMPAS and what was it designed to assess?
A: Show answer
COMPAS stands for Correctional Offender Management Profiling for Alternative Sanctions. The chapter describes it as a risk-and-needs assessment tool developed by Tim Brennan and Dave Wells. Its risk component uses a weighted combination of factors such as age, age at first arrest, and criminal history to estimate specified outcomes, including violent or nonviolent reoffending over an approximate one-to-three-year period. It also includes questions intended to identify needs such as chemical dependency, weak family support, and depression. The name's reference to alternative sanctions is significant: the instrument was presented as information that could help guide rehabilitation and noncustodial options, not simply as a mechanism for increasing imprisonment.
Study instruction:
- Separate the risk-prediction function from the needs-assessment function when describing COMPAS.
Key point:
- COMPAS combines risk estimates with information about needs.
- Its stated purpose included informing alternatives to incarceration.
7. Q: Why must a risk assessment distinguish violent reoffending, general reoffending, and failure to appear in court?
A: Show answer
These are different outcomes with different meanings and possible responses. A prediction about violent reoffending concerns a specified form of future harm. A prediction about general reoffending may depend on a broader set of offenses and on how those offenses become known to authorities. Failure to appear concerns compliance with a court date, not necessarily criminal conduct. Combining these outcomes into one vague label can conceal what is actually being predicted and encourage an inappropriate response. The chapter later explains that a person who may miss court could need a reminder or practical assistance rather than detention. Decision-makers should therefore inspect the target outcome before interpreting a score or selecting an intervention.
Study instruction:
- For each of the three outcomes, write one suitable intervention and explain why the same intervention should not automatically be applied to all.
Key point:
- Different predicted outcomes should not be treated as interchangeable.
- The response should fit the specific risk being estimated.
8. Q: What does a COMPAS score from 1 to 10 represent in the chapter's discussion?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a scale in which 1 represents the lowest risk category and 10 the highest. A score is an ordered risk estimate, not a direct observation of future behavior and not a moral judgment about the person. Northpointe argued that a given score had the same predictive meaning across racial groups, a property called calibration. That claim concerns how often people with a particular score experienced the measured outcome; it does not by itself settle whether the resulting decisions or error burdens are equitable. A score should therefore be interpreted with its target, validation evidence, uncertainty, and intended use clearly in view.
Study instruction:
- Explain what a score communicates and list at least three things it does not establish about an individual.
Key point:
- A risk score summarizes an estimate, not a certainty or character judgment.
- Its meaning depends on the outcome and calibration evidence.
9. Q: How did ProPublica investigate COMPAS in Broward County?
A: Show answer
Julia Angwin and colleagues obtained approximately eighteen thousand COMPAS scores from Broward County for 2013 and 2014 through a public-records request. To examine whether the scores corresponded to later outcomes, the team had to connect the assessment records to criminal-record data. That required extensive data cleaning and matching using names and dates of birth, complicated by spelling errors and inconsistent records. The investigation then compared score distributions and subsequent outcomes across Black and White defendants. The chapter emphasizes the labor involved in assembling the evidence: an audit of a consequential model may require access to outcome data, careful linkage, and independent analysis rather than reliance on vendor claims alone.
Study instruction:
- Describe the data sources needed to audit a predictive system and identify two data-quality problems that can undermine the audit.
Key point:
- Independent evaluation requires linking predictions to later outcomes.
- Poor data quality can make fairness analysis difficult and error-prone.
10. Q: Why were different score distributions across racial groups not sufficient by themselves to prove model bias?
A: Show answer
A difference in the distribution of scores is a warning that warrants investigation, but it does not alone reveal whether the model is misclassifying people. The groups might differ in the measured outcome prevalence, or the distribution might reflect other features in the data. To evaluate predictive performance, ProPublica needed to compare scores with subsequent records and examine which predictions were correct or incorrect. The chapter's account therefore distinguishes a descriptive disparity from evidence about error patterns and decision consequences. Neither score distributions nor aggregate accuracy should be treated as a complete fairness test in isolation.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish a group difference in score distribution from a group difference in false-positive or false-negative errors.
Key point:
- A disparity is a signal for investigation, not a complete diagnosis.
- Fairness analysis needs outcome data and a clearly stated metric.
11. Q: What is a false positive in a criminal-risk assessment?
A: Show answer
A false positive occurs when a person is classified as high risk but does not experience the outcome the model was intended to predict during the specified observation period. In the COMPAS debate described in the chapter, this included defendants rated as higher risk who were not subsequently rearrested for a new offense. If a high-risk classification contributes to detention or another restrictive decision, the error can impose serious costs on someone who would not have reoffended as measured. A false positive is not merely a technical inconvenience; its significance depends on the action attached to the score, the period observed, and the consequences for the individual.
Study instruction:
- Construct a simple two-by-two classification example and identify the false-positive cases.
Key point:
- A false positive is an incorrect high-risk classification.
- Its human cost depends on the decision made from the classification.
12. Q: What is a false negative in a criminal-risk assessment?
A: Show answer
A false negative occurs when a person is classified as low risk but later experiences the outcome the model was intended to predict. In the chapter's COMPAS discussion, this included defendants assigned lower risk who were later charged with new crimes. Such errors can matter greatly to public safety and to people harmed by subsequent offenses. However, a later event does not mean that every earlier release decision was necessarily unreasonable; the assessment is probabilistic and must be evaluated against the decision context and available alternatives. A fair evaluation should identify both false negatives and false positives rather than focusing on only one category.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between a false negative and a false positive, then describe who may bear the consequences of each.
Key point:
- A false negative is an incorrect low-risk classification.
- Public-safety concerns and individual liberty both matter when evaluating errors.
13. Q: What did ProPublica and Northpointe agree about COMPAS accuracy?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that the two sides agreed that COMPAS had approximately the same overall accuracy for Black and White defendants in the Broward County analysis, around 61 percent for predicting whether defendants would be rearrested. They disagreed about what the remaining errors meant for fairness. ProPublica emphasized that the types of mistakes were distributed differently between groups: Black defendants were more likely to be classified high risk without a subsequent rearrest, while White defendants were more likely to be classified low risk before a subsequent charge. This illustrates why a single overall accuracy figure can conceal consequential differences in who experiences each kind of error.
Study instruction:
- Explain why equal overall accuracy does not establish equal false-positive and false-negative rates.
Key point:
- The same aggregate accuracy can coexist with different error patterns.
- Fairness analysis must inspect the composition of errors.
14. Q: What is calibration in the context of risk prediction?
A: Show answer
Calibration means that a risk score has a consistent relationship with the observed outcome. If a score is calibrated across groups, people assigned the same score should experience the measured outcome at approximately the same rate, regardless of group membership. Northpointe argued that a COMPAS score such as 7 carried the same meaning for Black and White defendants. Calibration is valuable because it makes a score interpretable as a risk estimate rather than a group-specific label. But calibration does not guarantee equal false-positive rates, equal false-negative rates, equal access to favorable decisions, or equitable consequences. It is one fairness property, not a complete definition of fairness.
Study instruction:
- Write a plain-language definition of calibration and explain what comparison an auditor would make to test it.
Key point:
- Calibration compares observed outcomes among people with the same score.
- A calibrated model can still distribute errors unequally.
15. Q: Why did Northpointe argue that COMPAS was fair, and why did ProPublica disagree?
A: Show answer
Northpointe emphasized equal predictive accuracy across Black and White defendants and calibration, arguing that the same numerical score had the same meaning for both groups. ProPublica accepted that these properties were present in the data it examined but focused on the distribution of mistakes. It found that Black defendants were more likely to be false positives, while White defendants were more likely to be false negatives. The disagreement was therefore not simply about whether the calculations were correct. It concerned which statistical properties should define fairness and which harms should receive priority. The chapter uses the dispute to show that a fairness claim is incomplete unless its metric and moral rationale are made explicit.
Study instruction:
- Summarize each side's fairness argument in one sentence, then identify the metric each side prioritized.
Key point:
- The dispute concerned competing definitions, not just a disputed calculation.
- A fairness claim must specify which errors or outcomes it treats as important.
16. Q: Why is overall accuracy an incomplete measure of fairness?
A: Show answer
Overall accuracy combines correct and incorrect predictions into one summary number. Two groups can have the same proportion of correct predictions while experiencing different kinds of errors. In a high-stakes setting, those differences matter because false positives and false negatives can impose different burdens on different people. An aggregate figure may therefore hide whether one group is more likely to be unnecessarily restricted or whether another is more likely to experience an unanticipated harmful event. Accuracy remains useful for assessing predictive performance, but it should be accompanied by group-specific error rates, calibration checks, outcome definitions, and analysis of how scores affect actual decisions.
Study instruction:
- Design a short model-evaluation report with overall accuracy, calibration, false-positive rates, and false-negative rates by group.
Key point:
- Aggregate accuracy can conceal unequal error burdens.
- Multiple measures are needed to understand performance and fairness.
17. Q: Why can calibration and equal error rates conflict when groups have different base rates?
A: Show answer
